**T2.3 is folded in here rather than given its own decision, because it is the same question answered the other way.** That item asks that [D-060](s13-decision-register.md#d-060) — the overlap guard staying in the write actor — be revisited and *confirmed*, since §4.7 read as though it were unsettled. **Confirmed.** The actor now performs exactly the probe a trigger would, on an index built for it ([D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059)), with the statement prepared once ([D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064)); moving it into `trg_links_single_open` would add a second index probe to every insert on the path just made fast, to constrain writers who bypassed the actor — and a writer who can bypass a channel can bypass a `PRAGMA`. §4.7 now says "settled" where it said "open". The contrast with row 3 is the useful part: a `CHECK` was affordable precisely because the engine enforces it against the raw connection too, so it does not share row 1's "constrains only the people already behaving" problem, and it costs a comparison rather than an index probe.

Rejected: `CHECK (weight >= 0.0)` alone as specified (admits text, which panics on read, and infinity, which corrupts the log — only probing showed it); adding `AND weight IS NOT NULL` (`REAL NOT NULL` covers it, including NaN — [D-078](s13-decision-register.md#d-078)); deleting the loader guard now that the schema checks (it does not cover `links_current` or pre-v7 cold files); putting the same `CHECK` on `links_current` (it is derivative, rebuilt from `links` by trigger and by `rebuild_current`, so it would constrain the same data twice — and it would make `NegativeEdgeWeight` unreachable and untestable); clamping or dropping offending rows during migration (an edit to an assertion, which is the one thing this ledger does not do); deferring to 1.0 (the migration only gets more expensive, and D-036 makes it impossible).

<a id="d-084"></a>D-084 — `rowid_pk` on `concepts` is deferred to the release that implements erasure, with the trigger condition written down (0.6.0). [Doctrine V](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-v), [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036), [D-051](s13-decision-register.md#d-051), [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071), [D-083](s13-decision-register.md#d-083), [§4.6](s4-schema.md), [§5.9](s5-modules.md#59-vector--embeddings-the-model-registry-and-search). T2.2 proposes adding `rowid_pk INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` to `concepts` so that FTS5's `content_rowid` can point at a column `VACUUM` cannot renumber. The technical claim is sound and is **not** the reason this is deferred, so both halves are recorded here rather than left as an unexamined omission.

Sound: an `INTEGER PRIMARY KEY` *is* the rowid, so `VACUUM` cannot move it, and `content_rowid='rowid_pk'` would make [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071)'s dense-rowid argument unnecessary rather than merely true-for-now. Lookup by `id` costs the same either way — a `TEXT PRIMARY KEY` on a rowid table is already a unique index plus rowid indirection.

Deferred because it **does not unblock what it is for**. Erasure also needs the FTS delete trigger issuing FTS5's `'delete'` command with `OLD` values, which [§4.6](s4-schema.md) already names as the missing third trigger, and without that a deleted concept stays searchable. Taking `rowid_pk` alone buys nothing today and costs something: `AUTOINCREMENT`, which is what would guarantee non-reuse, writes a `sqlite_sequence` row on every concept insert, and non-reuse only matters once deletion exists. So the ordering is not "cheap thing first" but "the thing that is only correct as part of a set".

**Scheduled, with a condition rather than a version.** The first rung of whichever release implements concept archival or erasure carries: `rowid_pk`, the FTS delete trigger, and a `rebuild_fts()` call.

> **SUPERSEDED by [D-119](s13-decision-register.md#d-119) — DELIVERED in 0.8.0, ahead of the condition.** The condition was the wrong instrument: [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) forbids a primary-key change after 1.0, so waiting for the release that needs `rowid_pk` would have waited past the last release allowed to make it. 0.8.0 takes the whole set at once — `rowid_pk`, the delete trigger installed *inert*, and the FTS rebuild — which honours this entry's "only correct as part of a set" argument while paying the one rebuild it asks for. `AUTOINCREMENT` was still declined, on this entry's own reasoning. Note also that the technical premise recorded above as *sound* is **narrower than stated**: `VACUUM` would not in fact have renumbered v7's `concepts` — see [D-120](s13-decision-register.md#d-120). [D-083](s13-decision-register.md#d-083) is the precedent for why they travel together — SQLite has no `ADD CONSTRAINT` and no cheap `ALTER` on a table like this, so each separate visit to `concepts` is another full rebuild, and three changes in one rung cost one rebuild instead of three.

The counter-argument, stated because it is real: D-083 argues a schema change to a ledger table should be taken *now*, pre-1.0, while [D-032](s13-decision-register.md#d-032) makes it a cheap baseline re-issue and before [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036) freezes it. That applies here too, and if erasure lands after 1.0 this becomes expensive for exactly the reason D-083 gives. The difference is that D-083's constraint was independently correct — it closes a live panic and a live corruption path regardless of what ships next — whereas `rowid_pk` is inert until its companions exist. Adding an inert column to beat a deadline is how a schema accumulates fields nobody can explain. If 1.0 approaches with erasure still unscheduled, this decision should be revisited on the deadline argument alone, and that revisit is the obligation this entry exists to create.

Rejected: taking `rowid_pk` alone now (inert, and pays a hot-path `sqlite_sequence` write for a hazard [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071) measured as unreachable); `AUTOINCREMENT` without deletion (buys non-reuse nothing yet consumes); leaving it unrecorded until erasure is designed (an omission with no trigger condition is indistinguishable from an oversight, which is the failure mode this register exists to prevent).

<a id="d-085"></a>D-085 — A historical traversal must state which text it wants, and the shared hydrate chunk gets one definition (0.6.0). [Doctrine II](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-ii), [D-026](s13-decision-register.md#d-026), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [§5.2](s5-modules.md#52-graphbuilderrs--traversal-valid-time-and-attribute-fidelity), [§7.5](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md). `TraversalBuilder` returned the past's topology wearing the present's titles and reported that through `tracing::warn!`. A log line is not a boundary: it is invisible in any application that has not configured a subscriber, which is most of them at first run. This was the last silent-wrong-answer path the 0.5.6 cycle documented and did not close.

**Why it could only ever have been a warning before, which the item does not say.** `hydrate_attributes` takes the mode as a parameter and receives a `ts`, and a `ts` is just an instant — nothing in the call distinguishes "as of last Tuesday" from "as of now". So the warning fired on **every** `AttributeMode::Current` hydrate, overwhelmingly the ordinary live case where it is exactly right. Loud where it did not matter and, being a log line, silent where it did. No amount of upgrading `warn!` to an error at that layer would have worked; the information needed to raise one was never there.

So the fix is a representation change first. `TraversalBuilder::as_of(ts)` now exists, which makes "a query about the past" *expressible*; and `attribute_mode` became `Option<AttributeMode>`, where `None` means **defaulted** rather than `Current`. Those two carry exactly the information the decision needs. `execute` returns the new `DbError::AttributeModeUnstated` for the one combination that is a question — `as_of` set, mode never stated — and does what it is told for every other. The error text names both resolutions, because "you must choose" without saying between what is a worse boundary than a warning.

**The fast, mixed answer stays available.** Past topology with live text is a legitimate query — a caller rendering a historical diagram with today's labels wants precisely it — and the objection was always to receiving it without asking, never to asking for it. `.attribute_mode(AttributeMode::Current)` says so and is honoured.

**No existing caller changes.** A traversal with no `as_of` is a query about now, where `Current` and `AtTime` agree about which text to return, so the default stands. That is what keeps this from being a breaking change for everyone in order to fix one combination — the whole suite compiled and passed unaltered.

The test that pins it renames a concept *after* the instant being asked about, so the two modes genuinely disagree. Without that the fixture returns the same string under every mode and the question is invisible — which is precisely how this survived to 0.6.0. Writing it also surfaced Doctrine II inside the fixture: the topology filter is **valid** time while `AtTime` hydration is **transaction** time, and one `as_of` parameter feeds both, so the first version of the test failed by keying the rename on the wrong axis.

**T3.1 folded in.** `HYDRATE_CHUNK` was defined twice — `graph::subgraph` and `temporal::as_of`, both `400`, with the reasoning at one and `// See as_of::HYDRATE_CHUNK` at the other. It now lives in `util::limits` beside the `SQLITE_MAX_VARIABLE_NUMBER` it exists to stay under. The cross-reference comment was itself the evidence: the duplication was known and being managed by convention, and a shared constant is what replaces a convention. The margin check moved from a `#[test]` to a `const` block on clippy's prompting, which is strictly better — both sides are constants, and the failure it guards is someone tuning the value upward, plausibly in a release build without running the suite. `wave1_regression_tests` now derives its straddling fixture size from the constant rather than hardcoding 450, which would have silently stopped straddling anything the moment the constant moved.

Rejected: keeping the `warn!` alongside the error (it fires on the live path, where there is nothing to warn about); flipping the default to `AtTime` (correct at every instant, and it makes every live traversal read `transaction_log` instead of `concepts` — a performance regression on the common path to fix the rare one); comparing `ts` against the wall clock to detect a historical query (no API change needed, and it makes a correctness boundary depend on scheduling and on how stale the caller's `now` is); making `as_of` a required argument of `execute` (breaks every caller to reach the same place).

<a id="d-086"></a>D-086 — Pipelining the bulk paths was implemented, measured, and removed; the deduplication stayed (0.6.0). [D-011](s13-decision-register.md#d-011), [D-058](s13-decision-register.md#d-058), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [§5.1](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor). T3.4 observes that `bulk_import` and its three siblings `await` each chunk before sending the next, so the actor finishes a chunk and idles for a channel round trip before the following one arrives — ~11,000 such gaps on a 1M-edge import, with the 64-slot low-priority queue never more than one deep. It proposes sending *k* chunks ahead and estimates *k* = 2–4 captures most of the win.

**Every observation in that item is true and the conclusion does not follow.** The gaps are real and there are that many of them. They are also **four orders of magnitude smaller than the work they interrupt**: a tokio mpsc hop is sub-microsecond, and a chunk of [`chunk_rows::EDGES`](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor) takes 13–21 ms. The item sizes the win as though the round trip were comparable to the chunk cost.

Implemented and swept at depths 1, 2, 4, 8 and 16 over 20,000 and 100,000 edges, best of three, with the per-kind hold read from the actor's own counters ([D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079)):

| edges | depth 1 | depth 2 | depth 4 | depth 8 | depth 16 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 20,000 | 1,557 ms | 1,573 ms | 1,548 ms | 1,578 ms | 1,578 ms |
| 100,000 | 8,030 ms | 8,037 ms | 8,017 ms | 8,007 ms | 8,041 ms |

Ten cells, all within 1% of sequential, in both directions. The longest hold stayed at 13.8 ms and 21.0 ms throughout, which is the control the sweep exists to check: pipelining changes *when* chunks are sent and never how large they are, so a moving hold would have meant the change did something it should not have.

**It was not free to keep.** With chunks in flight, a failure at chunk *i* no longer leaves a **prefix** committed — chunks *i+1 … i+k−1* were already sent and commit anyway. [D-011](s13-decision-register.md#d-011) promises "a failure partway leaves earlier chunks committed", and a caller recovering from a partial import would no longer be able to assume "everything before the failure, nothing after". Paying for that with a weaker recovery story in exchange for nothing measurable is the wrong trade, so the pipelining came out.

**What stayed is the part that was worth doing anyway.** The four bulk paths had four copies of the same send-and-await loop; they now share `low_chunked`, which is sequential and stops sending at the first error, so what commits is exactly the prefix before the failure. `examples/pipeline_diag.rs` is kept deliberately — the item's reasoning is entirely plausible and will be re-proposed by the next reader of that loop, and the sweep is cheaper to re-run than the argument is to re-have.

Worth noting what this does *not* say. The measurement is against an **embedded, local** libSQL, where the channel hop and the storage are both in-process. A network-backed or replicated deployment would have a round trip that is not sub-microsecond, and there the item's reasoning would hold. That is a different engine profile, not a different chunk size, and it is the condition under which this decision should be revisited.

Rejected: keeping the pipelining at depth 4 because it is harmless (it is not — it weakens D-011's recovery guarantee, and a mechanism with no measured benefit is a mechanism that will not be maintained); keeping it at depth 1 (the machinery would be dead weight around a constant that makes it a no-op); leaving the four duplicate loops in place because the pipelining was rejected (the duplication was a real defect independent of the item that exposed it).

**Fixture: `chain` — the spine, without spurs ([D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), added 0.6.0).** `examples/pipeline_diag.rs` imports `c{i} -> c{i+1}` for 20,000 and 100,000 edges. Shape matters less here than in any other entry annotated in this pass, because the question is *when chunks are sent* rather than what is in them, and the control (the longest hold, which stayed flat at 13.8 ms and 21.0 ms) is what confirms that. It is stated anyway, and the one thing it changes is worth naming: a chain has out-degree 1, so the single-open probe ([D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059)) is at its cheapest here and the per-chunk cost is a floor rather than a typical figure.
<a id="d-087"></a>D-087 — `Subgraph` interning passes its own gate and is still deferred, on a cost the plan does not price; the measurement found a cheaper adjacent win (0.6.0). [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics). [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063) retired the integer-index rewrite on **CPU** grounds, and that measurement is sound: load dominates every algorithm at every size tested. T3.3 observes the **memory** argument was never made, and sets an explicit gate — measure *edges per budget*, not milliseconds, and skip if the budget is not what actually stops real callers.

**The gate is passed.** Measured by the loader's own accounting (`examples/budget_density_diag.rs`), an edge costs two adjacency entries because `add_edge` stores a full `EdgeRef` in `out_adj` and a clone in `in_adj`, each carrying an owned id and two owned 27-byte timestamps:

| id length | bytes/edge now | interned | edges per MiB now | interned | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | 342 | 48 | 3,066 | 21,845 | **7.1×** |
| 26 (ULID) | 378 | 48 | 2,774 | 21,845 | **7.9×** |
| 64 | 454 | 48 | 2,309 | 21,845 | **9.5×** |

`size_of::<EdgeRef>()` alone is 104 bytes — four `String` headers and an `f64` — before a single byte of payload. Interned to `{u32, u32, f64, u32, u32}` it is 24 with no heap payload at all. This is a **reachability** improvement, the category [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073) used, and it needs no schema change.

**Deferred anyway, on a cost T3.3 does not price.** `Subgraph`, `EdgeRef` and `NodeData` are public types with public fields, read directly by every algorithm and constructed directly by tests and callers. Interning is not an internal representation change; it is a breaking change to the crate's main read-side data structure, and 0.6.0 is a hardening cycle whose other items are deliberately additive or invisible. Scheduled for 0.7.0 with the API break named, rather than smuggled into a release that promised not to move anything. **RESCHEDULED from 0.7.0.** It did not ship there, and this sentence went on saying it would for a whole release. 0.7.0 became the Python bindings release; nothing here was interned, and nothing anywhere went red, because a scheduled decision is prose ([D-113](s13-decision-register.md#d-113) is the tripwire that ends that). Now scheduled for **0.8.0**, item B2, and the premise is stronger than it was: [D-101](s13-decision-register.md#d-101)'s opaque `Subgraph` means the Python surface does not move, and 0.7.0 has no downloads, so the only argument for deferring the break has expired.

**The measurement also found something cheaper, which is the part worth acting on first.** The same sweep asked where the budget actually goes, at 20 edges per node:

| content bytes/concept | node bytes | edge bytes | edges' share |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 37,800 | 1,368,000 | 97% |
| 200 | 77,800 | 1,368,000 | 95% |
| 2,000 | 437,800 | 1,368,000 | 76% |
| 20,000 | 4,037,800 | 1,368,000 | 25% |

So interning wins big exactly when concepts are small, and is swamped when they are large. And `load_subgraph_with` **always** hydrates `content` — its rustdoc says so plainly, "`attribute_mode` is ignored" — while **no graph algorithm reads it**: `dijkstra`, `astar`, `louvain`, `k_core`, `scc` and `modularity` touch topology and weights only. A caller running Louvain over a corpus of 20 KB documents is spending three quarters of the byte budget on document bodies that the algorithm will not look at.

That is a smaller change than interning, in the opposite regime, and the two are complementary rather than alternatives: dropping `content` helps when nodes dominate, interning helps when edges do. Recorded here rather than acted on because it is a new finding rather than a plan item, and because `Subgraph` carrying live concept text is a documented property that some caller may be relying on — the fix is a load option, not a silent change of what the type contains.

Rejected: doing the interning now (a public API break in a hardening release); keying it on `rowid_pk` as an earlier proposal did (sparse and unbounded, so it still needs a map rather than a vector — and it would drag in [D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084)'s deferred schema change); re-litigating [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063) (its CPU finding is not disputed and this does not rest on it); dropping `content` from `Subgraph` unconditionally as part of this decision (it is what the type has always carried, and changing that quietly is the failure mode this register exists to prevent).

**Fixture: none — measured on `Subgraph`'s own byte accounting ([D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), added 0.6.0).** `examples/budget_density_diag.rs` computes bytes per edge from `size_of::<EdgeRef>()` and the loader's own formula, and builds its node-share table by hand at a fixed 20 edges per node. No database and no graph shape is involved, which is why the conclusion generalises where a timing would not — the arithmetic is the same on every shape, and only the *edges-per-node* ratio that decides whether it matters is shape-dependent. `dense_small` is where it matters most and `chain` where it matters least.
<a id="d-088"></a>D-088 — The fixture matrix, and the shape a figure was taken on becomes part of the figure (0.6.0). [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070), [D-076](s13-decision-register.md#d-076), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets). T4.1 is the plan's own "most important item", on the grounds that one fixture shape produced [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070)'s wrong conclusion, T0.1's invisibility, and [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059)'s still-open note that the chunk constants are empty-database figures needing "a realistic fixture, which requires deciding what 'realistic' means." Four shapes now exist in `tests/fixtures.rs`, each named for the cost it is the worst case for: `star_of_stars` (hub out-degree; the existing one), `clustered` (path enumeration, Louvain), `chain` (recursion depth, snapshot fold length), `dense_small` (the `DISTINCT` sort, the byte budget). `benches/budgets.rs` no longer carries its own copy of the star — `seed_edges` delegates to `Shape::StarOfStars`, so the shape every pre-0.6.0 §9 figure was taken on is now a named member of the matrix rather than an anonymous local function.

**The headline number, which is the item's whole case.** `load_subgraph` at *comparable coverage* — each shape at the depth it needs to reach 90% of itself, so the rows are the same size of problem:

| shape | depth | nodes | load |
|---|---|---|---|
| `star_of_stars` | 2 | 600 | **3.06 ms** |
| `chain` | 485 | 541 | 23.2 ms |
| `clustered` | 89 | 541 | 235 ms |
| `dense_small` | 1 | 300 | 259 ms |

§9's `load_subgraph` budget is a measurement of the first row. The spread across shapes at comparable size is **77×**, and none of it was visible before.

**Coverage, not depth, and this correction is the part T4.1 does not contain.** The obvious way to build the table is to fix the depth, and the first version did. At a fixed depth of 3 the four shapes reach **600, 25, 6 and 300** nodes — so that table compares a 600-node problem against a 6-node one and reports the difference as a property of the shape. That is [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070)'s error committed inside the file written to prevent it: a frame nobody checked. `depth_to_cover` exists so a shape-crossing measurement has to say which variable it holds fixed. Depth is the right control for "what does one more hop cost"; coverage is the right control for "what does this shape cost at comparable size", and the two answer different questions.

**A second correction, to my own first draft.** `Facts` initially called its path count "the CTE's row count". It is not: T0.1 replaced `UNION ALL` + a `path` column with a plain `UNION`, which dedupes on `(node_id, depth)` and bounds the walk at `reached × (depth+1)`. The number is retained and renamed `simple_paths`, with the caveat stated on the type, because it remains the metric that separates a tree from a graph — and because it is the cost that *returns* the instant path semantics are reintroduced, which T0.1's own rustdoc argues is a live risk since the two forms have equal reachability and every test passes under either. Reported beside `union_bound`, their ratio is what T0.1 bought: **22,127× on `dense_small`** (26,552,696 simple paths against a bound of 1,200) and **0.25× on `star_of_stars`** — the only fixture that existed when T0.1 was written.

**`clustered` had to be rebuilt once, and the reason is the finding in miniature.** The first version linked each community as `i -> j, i < j`, which reads as dense and is a **DAG**: path length is bounded by community size and the walk stays near-linear. Measured at 9×, it would have shipped as a weaker `star_of_stars` under a name promising a different question. Both directions make each community strongly connected — what Louvain and `scc` are actually looking for — and take the same walk to 47×. `fixture_matrix_tests` pins that, along with the other property each shape was chosen for, and `the_matrix_discriminates` asserts the four *differ* from each other, because four fixtures that behave alike are one fixture.

**The rule this exists to enforce**: a performance decision entry names the shape it was measured on. [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070)'s would have read "inherent on `star_of_stars`", and the gap would have been a sentence in the entry rather than a wave.

Rejected: putting the fixtures in `src` behind a feature (dev-only code in the shipped library, and a `fixtures` feature in `[[bench]] required-features` makes a plain `cargo bench` skip the benchmarks silently — the path-include follows `tests/harness.rs`, whose cost is one empty test binary); scaling `dense_small` with the bench scale factor (edge count is `n(n-1)`, so it is minutes of fixture construction at any interesting node count, and the shape's point is density at a *small* node count — capped at 300 and pinned as a stated property); changing `star_of_stars` while moving it (every pre-0.6.0 figure is on that arithmetic and altering it would orphan the record); deriving `Facts::reached` from the path enumeration in the depth sweep (`O(simple_paths)`, which is 26.5 million at depth 3 on `dense_small` and unbounded above it — `reach_by_depth` is one BFS).

**The rule was applied prospectively, and that was half the item (added later in 0.6.0).** The matrix and the rule landed together and only the *new* entries named their fixture, which left nine older performance decisions reading as though their figures were general — exactly the condition this entry exists to end, one level up. They are annotated now: [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063), [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064), [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070), [D-076](s13-decision-register.md#d-076), [D-086](s13-decision-register.md#d-086), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087). Three things came out of doing it that arguing about it would not have produced. [D-063](s13-decision-register.md#d-063) **already named its fixture** ("on the star-of-stars fixture, three hops from the root"), which is why its CPU finding has survived unchallenged where [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070)'s did not. [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055) is the largest single instance: all twelve §9 budget rows, the project's public statement of what it costs, on one shape. And [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087) needed an **opt-out** rather than a shape — it was measured on `Subgraph`'s own byte accounting with no database at all, which is why the rule now admits `**Fixture: none — …**` as an answer that still requires a sentence.

`every_performance_decision_names_its_fixture` in `tests/fixture_matrix_tests.rs` keeps the annotations from rotting, and caught [D-086](s13-decision-register.md#d-086) — written in this same cycle and already unannotated — on its first run. Its limitation is stated in the test rather than hidden: the list of performance entries is hand-maintained, so a *newly added* one is not detected. What is enforced is that an existing annotation cannot quietly disappear and cannot name a shape the matrix lacks, since the accepted names come from `Shape::name` rather than a second list. The first version matched the bare word and accepted "chain" out of "snapshot chain"; it matches backticked names only, because a tripwire that fires on prose is one that gets disabled.
<a id="d-089"></a>D-089 — Plan-pinning becomes a registry keyed by index, which found two indexes with no reader (0.6.0). [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059), [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064), [§4.2](s4-schema.md#42-links-assertion-history-and-current-belief-materialization), [§4.5](s4-schema.md#45-analytics-annotations--the-second-derivative-table-054-d-041), [§8](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#8-testing-strategy). T4.2 observes that [D-042](s13-decision-register.md#d-042), [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059) and [D-064](s13-decision-register.md#d-064) are one bug three times — *a covering index captures a query because it contains the columns, not because it discriminates* — each found by measurement after it shipped, each answered with one more reactive `EXPLAIN` test. It proposes the rule: every query with an index dependency ships with a plan assertion.

**Implemented in the other direction, and that is what made it find something.** `tests/index_plan_tests.rs` is keyed by *index*, not by query: every entry in `ddl::CREATE_INDICES` must name the query that justifies it, and `every_index_is_justified` fails if an index is added without one. The query-keyed form catches a query that leaves its index — which is what the three reactive tests do, and they are kept. It cannot catch an index that **no query ever seeks on**, because there is no query to write an assertion against. That is pure cost: an index write on every insert into its table, forever, read by nothing.

**Two of the six are exactly that.**

- `idx_annotations_label` — nothing in the crate selects from `analytics_annotations` at all. It is written by `write_analytics_annotations` and read only by callers, through their own connection.
- `idx_lc_tgt_active (target_id, valid_to)` — no query in the crate seeks on `target_id` as a leading column. Every `links_current` predicate either leads on `source_id` (the walk) or binds all three key columns (the two guards), and `Subgraph::in_adj` is built in Rust from the forward rows the walk already returned.

Verified rather than inferred: `examples/index_coverage_probe.rs` confirms both indexes *would* be chosen by a query of the obvious shape, so this is "nothing runs that query", not "the planner declines the index". The cost is not hypothetical — [D-059](s13-decision-register.md#d-059)'s own note weighs "a fourth index write per assertion" as a price worth arguing about, and `idx_lc_tgt_active` is one of those four on the crate's hottest write path.

**Recorded and scheduled rather than dropped.** Removing an index is a schema change needing a `DROP INDEX` rung, in a release whose other items are additive or invisible — the same reason [D-084](s13-decision-register.md#d-084) and [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087) were deferred. `the_unread_indices_are_the_two_already_known` pins the set, so a third joining them is a red test rather than a line of DDL nobody revisits. Scheduled for 0.7.0 alongside the other schema work. **RESCHEDULED from 0.7.0.** There was no schema work in 0.7.0 to put it alongside — the release was the Python bindings — and the sentence stayed as written until [D-113](s13-decision-register.md#d-113) made it a failing test. Now scheduled for **0.8.0**, item B4, which is the `v7 → v8` rung: the two indices are dropped there together with the `rowid_pk` change. **DELIVERED in 0.8.0** — see [D-118](s13-decision-register.md#d-118), which also carries the measured cost of having kept them (−7.9% off `assert_edge`) and replaces `the_unread_indices_are_the_two_already_known` with `the_unread_index_set_is_empty`. Note also that this entry and `README.md` had both come to describe the pair as *added in v8*; they are in the **v7 baseline** — in `ddl::CREATE_INDICES`, created on every fresh database — and v8 is what will remove them.

The registry reproduces private query constants, which is the hazard `migration_tests` already documented for trigger bodies. `every_reproduced_query_still_exists_in_its_source` bounds it by `include_str!`ing the module each query came from and matching a fragment, whitespace-normalised because the sources are CRLF — compile-time, no widened API surface, and red when the original moves.

Rejected: making the fold and archive SQL constants `pub` so the test could explain the real string (widens the public surface to serve a test, when `include_str!` gets the same guarantee); dropping the two indexes in this release (a schema rung, and [D-036](s13-decision-register.md#d-036)'s freeze deserves the same care an addition gets); leaving them undocumented because they might be useful to a future query (an index whose justification is a query nobody has written is the definition of the cost this entry names).

<a id="d-090"></a>D-090 — The bench control row is enforced by the group constructor rather than by a convention (0.6.0). [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-055](s13-decision-register.md#d-055), [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070), [§9](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#9-performance-budgets). [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070) measured ~29% session-to-session noise in this project's absolute timings and recorded the consequence as a caveat. A caveat is only as good as the next reader's memory of it, and the failure is silent: a 20% regression and a 20% quiet machine print identically. T4.3 proposes a fixed trivial operation as row zero of every bench group.

Taken, with one change of form. T4.3 says "add a control row to every group", and a rule of that shape is followed until the next group is added in a hurry — so the control lives in the **constructor**: `controlled_group` is the only way to obtain a `BenchmarkGroup` in `budgets.rs`, and it has already added the row before it returns. `tests/bench_control_tests.rs` keeps the back door shut with three assertions, the third of which matters more than it looks: the control must have exactly one spelling, or two criterion series appear and a baseline comparison silently compares a group against a control it did not have last time.

The control is a `SELECT 1` round trip on a process-wide in-memory connection, not a CPU loop: the noise being corrected for is thermal state, the scheduler *and* libSQL's own overhead, and a hashing loop tracks the first of those and none of the rest.

**Measured, on the first run that used it.** Two back-to-back `traversal` runs on a quiet machine:

| | run 1 | run 2 | absolute | as a ratio to control |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `control/select_1` | 1.639 µs | 1.589 µs | −3.0% | — |
| `three_hop_warm` | 1.704 ms | 1.635 ms | −4.0% | **−1.0%** |
| `as_of_edges` | 720.3 µs | 700.0 µs | −2.8% | **−0.2%** |

The control absorbs most of the drift, which is the claim. This is a quiet pair of runs rather than the cross-session case [D-070](s13-decision-register.md#d-070) measured at 29%, so it demonstrates the mechanism rather than bounding it.

What this does not do is normalise the numbers automatically — criterion measures each row independently and the division is still the reader's. The value is that the divisor is now *present in the same run*, which is what makes the ratio computable at all; converting the caveat into an enforced row was the item's stated goal and is what was taken.

Rejected: a pure-CPU control (misses IO and engine overhead, which is where this project's noise mostly lives); one control per file rather than per group (criterion groups can run in any order and separately via a filter, so a per-file control is not in the same measurement window as most of what it would normalise); post-processing `target/criterion` into a ratio table (a second tool to maintain, and the numbers it would consume are already printed).

<a id="d-091"></a>D-091 — `diagnostic_conn()` is a boundary where `read_conn()` is a guardrail, and `raw()` becomes undiscoverable rather than unavailable (0.6.0). [D-019](s13-decision-register.md#d-019), [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050), [D-068](s13-decision-register.md#d-068), [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069), [§4.7](s4-schema.md#47-what-this-schema-does-not-enforce), [§5.1](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor). T5.1 begins by pointing out that a read-only diagnostic connection **already exists** — `read_conn()` carries `PRAGMA query_only = ON` — and that the real question is therefore only what to do about `raw()`. It then makes the sharper point: `query_only` is per-connection and reversible by its holder in one statement, so it is a guardrail and not a capability boundary, and `read_conn()` hands back a *shared* `&Connection`, so it does not serve a caller who wants their own.

**Both halves of that were measured rather than accepted** (`examples/readonly_open_probe.rs`, libSQL 0.9.30, against a live WAL database with the actor running):

| | `read_conn()` | `diagnostic_conn()` |
|---|---|---|
| `SELECT`, `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` | allowed | allowed |
| `INSERT` | refused | refused |
| `PRAGMA query_only = OFF` | allowed | allowed |
| `INSERT` after that | **allowed** | **refused** |

The last row is the entire difference and the reason the new method exists. Rows two and three would look identical for a second `query_only` connection, so `tests/diagnostic_conn_tests.rs` asserts the pair in **both** directions: turning the pragma off must restore writes on the reader (or §4.7 invariant 2 is describing something else) and must not on the diagnostic connection.

**One finding the item does not anticipate, and it runs the other way.** `CREATE TEMP TABLE` **succeeds** on a `SQLITE_OPEN_READ_ONLY` connection and is refused by `read_conn()` — temp tables live in a separate writable temporary database, whereas `query_only` refuses them outright. That is precisely the mechanism [D-050](s13-decision-register.md#d-050) measured when it removed `TwoPhaseTempTable` for returning `SQLITE_READONLY (8)` on the read connection. So the stronger boundary is not uniformly stronger, and one of D-050's two reasons for removing that strategy no longer applies to every connection this crate can offer. Recorded, not acted on: D-050 had a second reason (`vector_top_k` refuses a fourth argument and there is no allow-list to push into) and that one is untouched.

**`raw()` is `#[doc(hidden)]`, and the `raw-access` feature T5.1 also offers was rejected on a specific cost.** A feature is the stronger declaration — it appears in the consumer's `Cargo.toml`, where a reviewer sees it. But Cargo features are additive and cannot be *required* by a test target except through `required-features`, which makes a plain `cargo test` **skip** that binary silently. The binaries that call `raw()` are `storage_boundary_tests` and `wave1_regression_tests`: the §4.7 tripwires, whose whole job is to fail when a documented gap moves. Gating them would mean the ordinary `cargo test` stopped running the tests that enforce the section this item is about, in order to make a declaration about a hatch — the same "suite quietly does less than it appears to" failure the project already names for R15 reporting. So the hatch stays reachable and stops being discoverable, and the legitimate-use list in its rustdoc shrinks from three items to one: *provoking a guard*. [D-068](s13-decision-register.md#d-068) is unchanged; removing `raw()` would still buy the appearance of a guarantee, since the file is reachable by any SQLite client on the machine.

A missing file gets its own error variant rather than `NotFound`, which renders "node {0} not found" — [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069) is this project's record of what an error naming the wrong subject costs, and a database file is not a node. `SQLITE_OPEN_READ_ONLY` drops `SQLITE_OPEN_CREATE` with it, so a missing path is `SQLITE_CANTOPEN` rather than a fresh empty database — the right behaviour and an opaque thing to receive. The test for it asserts the **rendering and not the branch**, said plainly in the test rather than glossed: deleting the file underneath an open `Database` fails with OS error 32 on Windows (verified) and `close()` consumes the handle, so no in-process sequence reaches that branch.

Rejected: the `raw-access` feature (above); making `raw()` private ([D-068](s13-decision-register.md#d-068), unchanged); returning a `&Connection` from `diagnostic_conn` and caching one on the handle (the point is that the caller gets *their own*, so a long reporting query does not compete with every traversal in the process); reusing `NotFound` for the missing file; closing §4.7 invariant 2 on the strength of this (it closes one of three holes — the free `register_model` / `upsert_embedding` still take a bare connection, and the file is still reachable by any client).

<a id="d-092"></a>D-092 — R15's load-bearing claim is defended by a soak with a positive control, and the snapshot chain gets a cross-check that reports rather than repairs (0.6.0). [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi), [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030), [D-043](s13-decision-register.md#d-043), [D-049](s13-decision-register.md#d-049), [D-053](s13-decision-register.md#d-053), [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071), [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#r15), [§5.5](s5-modules.md#55-temporalreplayrs-and-temporalsnapshotrs--reconstruction-and-snapshots).

**T5.2 — the soak.** R15's mitigation rests on *"production exposure is nil by construction — an application opens one `Database` and holds it for its lifetime"*, and nothing tested it. The plan already sharpens the claim (`open_inner` opens three connections, four with the cadence, so the honest statement is **one process, one file, a bounded set of connections opened once and never churned**) and already requires a **control arm**, without which a clean result cannot be told apart from a harness that provoked nothing.

*The instrument had to be a subprocess runner, and that is the part worth recording.* The fault is a process-level access violation: not a panic, not a SQLite error, nothing to catch and nothing to assert on. A `#[test]` that provoked it would take its whole binary down and be reported — as the plan itself documents — as *fewer passing tests and no failures*. So `examples/r15_soak.rs` re-executes itself with `--child` and tallies exit codes, `-1073741819` being `0xC0000005`.

| arm | shape | result |
|---|---|---|
| `claim` | one long-lived `Database`, cadence on, 16 concurrent readers running `load_subgraph` + `reconstruct`, actor saturated | **0 / 10** at 15 s, **0 / 6** at 60 s |
| `control` | the same, plus a task opening 48 databases concurrently in the same process | **2 / 10** at 15 s |

About 8.7 minutes of continuous load in the claimed-safe shape with no fault, in a session where the control faulted twice — both within the first three seconds, which is when the open storm ramps. **The claim is defended and can now be cited**, in its sharpened form. It is a rate and not a proof, and the trigger is still concurrent *open*.

**Filed upstream 2026-07-31** by the maintainer, against libSQL 0.9.30, from the reproduction written here; the issue reference is not recorded in this document. Through 0.6.0 this paragraph read *"still not filed - filing it needs credentials this project's tooling does not hold"*, which was true when written and is not now.

**T5.3 — the snapshot chain.** `write_final` calls `reconstruct`, which composes onto the previous snapshot whenever one is usable, so snapshot *n* descends from snapshot *n−1* and nothing in the chain ever folds the whole log. An error at any link is copied forward indefinitely and every read agrees with it, because every read descends from it. `verify_snapshot_chain` folds from genesis — by withholding the snapshot directory, so `snapshot_anchor` finds nothing and the second computation is genuinely independent — and compares.

*It reports and does not repair, and that is a decision rather than caution.* Under [Doctrine VI](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-vi) a snapshot is derivative and disposable, so the repair is *delete the snapshot directory*: one line, available to the caller, correct without this function's help, and pinned by `deleting_the_snapshots_restores_agreement`. What the caller cannot get for themselves is the knowledge that the chain diverged, and rewriting the file would destroy the only evidence that composition has a defect. A divergence is a wrong **cache**, not a corrupt ledger, and the report says so.

*Not scheduled by this crate.* A genesis fold is exactly the cost snapshots exist to avoid, on a log large enough for snapshots to matter — the only case where this is worth running. Putting it in the cadence would hand every application that bill at a moment nobody chose, on a connection shared with nothing. The plan calls it a scheduling problem and the schedule is the caller's.

*The load-bearing test is the tampered one.* A checker only ever seen to pass is the exact shape this project keeps finding defects in — [D-030](s13-decision-register.md#d-030)'s `audit_current` reduced to a constant zero and certified any corruption as clean; [D-071](s13-decision-register.md#d-071)'s FTS `'integrity-check'` reports healthy on an emptied index. So `a_tampered_snapshot_is_caught` writes a **plausible** wrong snapshot — correctly serialised, correctly named, correctly anchored, with one title altered, one concept removed and one edge dropped — and requires the report to name all three. Not a corrupt file, which zstd or bincode would notice; the kind nothing does.

Two comparison details that would otherwise be silent bugs: `seq_anchor` is reported and **never compared**, because the composed answer legitimately anchors at the snapshot it started from plus its delta while the fold anchors at the newest row it saw; and edges are compared as a **set**, because `MaterializedState::edges` is a `Vec` with no declared order and a reordering is not a divergence — a false positive here is worse than useless, since the whole meaning of a report is "go and find the bug".

Rejected: running the cross-check on the cadence (above); repairing on divergence (destroys the evidence, and the caller already has a correct one-line repair); comparing `MaterializedState` with `PartialEq` (would report anchor and ordering differences as divergences); returning a bool (a divergence needs the disagreeing rows to be actionable); an unbounded report (a chain that went wrong early disagrees about every row — capped at `ChainCheck::SAMPLE_LIMIT` with a `truncated` flag, pinned by `the_report_is_bounded`).

<a id="d-093"></a>D-093 — The Python wheel is built with the `metrics` feature on (0.7.0). [D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079), [§14.1](s14-python-bindings.md#141-build-topology).

`metrics` is off by default in Rust for a reason that does not survive the boundary: a Rust consumer who wants the write actor's latency counters turns the feature on. **A Python consumer cannot.** A Cargo feature does not survive into a binary wheel, so anything the wheel omits is unavailable downstream permanently — not a default to change, but a capability that does not exist.

[T1.4 / D-079](s13-decision-register.md#d-079)'s argument was that a `CHUNK_BUDGET` which cannot be checked in situ is an aspiration rather than a bound. The wheel is the one binding that could not opt in later, so it is the one where that argument is decisive. The cost is the one D-079 already priced: a clock read per actor turn, on a build where `HoldTimer` is not a ZST.

Rejected: shipping two wheels, one with counters and one without — a matrix that doubles for a per-turn clock read, and a support question ("which wheel do you have?") on every latency report.

<a id="d-094"></a>D-094 — The wheel is `abi3-py310`: one wheel per platform rather than one per Python minor version (0.7.0). [§14.5](s14-python-bindings.md#145-embeddings-and-abi3).

`libsql-ffi` compiles the SQLite amalgamation from source for every target, so the wheel matrix is the dominant build cost and abi3 divides it by roughly five. `py310` rather than an older floor because each one forgoes API for users who are not there.

**The justification recorded when this was decided was wrong, and compiling found it.** It read *"the price is the limited C API, which these bindings do not touch"*. They touch it in two places, both compiled out under `Py_LIMITED_API`: `PyDateAccess` / `PyTimeAccess`, pyo3's struct accessors for datetime fields, and `pyo3::buffer`, which is the whole of the buffer protocol.

The consequences are absorbed rather than worked around. Timestamp fields are read with `getattr` — seven Python lookups instead of seven struct reads, on the coercion path only. `isoformat()` is the tempting single-call alternative and is a trap: it omits `.000000` when microseconds are zero, so every timestamp landing exactly on a second would render non-canonical, and only *some* values would be wrong, which is the worst way for a format defect to behave. The buffer protocol is replaced by an explicit packed-`bytes` path.

**Re-decided on measurement rather than left standing on the broken justification.** Coercing a 768-dimension vector: packed bytes 60.8 µs, numpy `float32` as a sequence 94.9 µs, numpy `float64` 114.3 µs, Python list 73.5 µs. The buffer protocol would have bought ~35 µs per vector — 1.6×, not the order of magnitude the original reasoning implied — against a 4–5× matrix. abi3 stands.

Revisit if P4.4 measures the sequence path as a real cost against DiskANN insertion; the exit is dropping abi3, which is a wheel-matrix decision and not a local one.

<a id="d-095"></a>D-095 — The Python binding is synchronous, over one process-wide runtime, with the GIL released for every call that reaches the engine (0.7.0). [D-028](s13-decision-register.md#d-028), [§5.1](s5-modules.md#51-connectionrs--the-handle-the-pragmas-and-the-write-actor), [§14.2](s14-python-bindings.md#142-the-async-sync-boundary).

An asyncio-native surface is rejected on the architecture's own grounds rather than on taste: the Write Actor is the sole write connection and serialises every write through one channel, so `await` on the write path would advertise concurrency the design does not grant. The read path is genuinely concurrent, but a surface where some methods are awaitable and some are not is worse than either pure form.

**One runtime, in a `OnceLock`, never dropped.** `Runtime::drop` panics when it runs inside a runtime context, and a runtime owned by a Python object is dropped wherever the collector decides. A static is never dropped, so the question does not arise; the cost is idle threads outliving the last handle.

**`Database: Send + Sync` is what the whole design rests on** — it is what `Python::detach` requires, and without it the only build that compiles is `#[pyclass(unsendable)]`, which pins the object to its creating thread and makes GIL release unavailable. Asserted at compile time, so a future field on `Database` breaks the binding with an explanation rather than inside a macro expansion.

Three consequences were found by building it rather than by planning it. `close()` consumes `self`, which Python cannot express, so the handle holds an `Option` and every other method meets a `None`. The class is `#[pyclass(frozen)]` over an `RwLock` rather than taking `&mut self`: a non-frozen class borrows through a runtime `RefCell`, and that borrow is live across the whole GIL-released call, so a second thread entering any method gets `PyBorrowMutError` — an error about pyo3's internals, raised for an ordinary concurrent read. And the lock must be taken *inside* the GIL-released closure, or `close()` blocking on the write lock while holding the GIL deadlocks against a reader that needs the GIL back to finish.

[D-028](s13-decision-register.md#d-028)'s rule carries across unchanged and is documented where a Python caller will look: awaiting a write waits on a Rust channel, not in SQLite, so a timeout is not a cancellation — running a write on a thread and abandoning it leaves the command queued.

Rejected: `pyo3-async-runtimes` (above); a runtime per handle; making `fork()` work — the `os.register_at_fork` guard converts a silent hang into an exception that names the cause, which is the honest ceiling.

<a id="d-096"></a>D-096 — Timestamps cross as `str` or aware `datetime` inbound and `datetime` outbound; an **open interval is `None`** (0.7.0). [Doctrine II](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-ii), [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029), [§4.1](s4-schema.md#41-concepts-and-per-model-embeddings), [§14.4](s14-python-bindings.md#144-timestamps-and-value-types).

[D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029) fixes every temporal column at `YYYY-MM-DDTHH:MM:SS.ffffffZ` and refuses anything else rather than guessing. That is the first thing a Python caller trips over, because they will pass a `datetime`.

**A naive datetime is refused, not assumed to be UTC.** D-029's rule one layer out: a naive value does not say which instant it names, and choosing one for the caller is a wrong answer in a temporal query later, shifted by an amount nothing records. A bare `date` is refused for the same reason — which midnight, in which zone, is exactly what it does not answer.

**The open sentinel was going to be a module-level `datetime` constant, and measurement refuted that.** `9999-12-31T23:59:59.999999Z` is exactly `datetime.max`, so it is representable — and unusable. Measured on CPython 3.13: `astimezone(timezone(timedelta(hours=1)))` raises `OverflowError`, `astimezone()` with no argument raises `OSError`, and adding one microsecond raises `OverflowError`. Rendering an instant in local time is among the most ordinary things anyone does with an aware datetime, and it fails for every zone east of UTC.

Under [Doctrine II](s0-s3-foundations.md#doctrine-ii) the open interval is *current belief*, so those are not edge-case rows; they are the common ones. A landmine in the common path is worse than a less convenient type. `None` says what is true — an open interval has no end — is symmetric on the way in, and cannot be confused with "missing", because every interval has a `valid_to`. `macrame.OPEN` is exported as the stored string for callers who need to name it.

The cost is real and stated rather than hidden: `None` does not compare with `datetime`, so sorting a `valid_to` column needs `key=lambda r: (r.valid_to is None, r.valid_to)`. The probe is kept as a test, so a future CPython that makes those operations work fails it and signals that this can be revisited.

Rejected: a `datetime` subclass overriding `astimezone` to return itself (a lie that reads as a value); assuming UTC for naive input; widening a `date` to midnight.

<a id="d-097"></a>D-097 — `Subgraph` crosses to Python as an opaque handle, not as a dict (0.7.0). **Decided; lands with P4.2.** [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087), [§5.4](s5-modules.md#54-graphsubgraphrs-and-graphalgorithmsrs--native-in-memory-analytics), [§14.9](s14-python-bindings.md#149-status).

`Subgraph` is three `BTreeMap`s bounded by an explicit byte budget because it is already the largest thing the crate materialises. Converting it into Python dicts on return **doubles the peak memory of the one operation that already carries a budget**, and does it eagerly whether or not the caller reads more than `degree()`.

So it is a `#[pyclass]` wrapping the Rust value with the accessors forwarded, plus an explicit `.to_dict()` for callers who want the copy and have decided to pay for it.

Recorded here ahead of implementation because it constrains P4.2's shape, and because [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087)'s interning work would otherwise be planned against a boundary that had already discarded its benefit.

<a id="d-098"></a>D-098 — The bindings are a workspace *leaf*; the root package does not move (0.7.0). [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), [§3](s0-s3-foundations.md#3-crate-layout), [§14.1](s14-python-bindings.md#141-build-topology).

The brief was a single `Cargo.toml` with the Rust crate still buildable separately and cleanly from the wheel. The second half is the requirement; the first does not survive three facts. `crate-type` is not feature-conditional in Cargo, so one manifest means a permanent `cdylib` relinking the `libsql-ffi` amalgamation on every release build and advertised to every consumer of `macrame-db`. `pyo3/extension-module` must be on for the wheel and off for `cargo test`, and CI's `cargo check --all-features --all-targets` turns on every feature by definition. And `[lib] name = "macrame"` is already load-bearing — one manifest cannot also name the extension module.

**The conventional split — move the crate to `crates/macrame/` — is unavailable here, and the reason is specific to this repository.** `tests/fixture_matrix_tests.rs` reaches `docs/architecture/s13-decision-register.md` through `include_str!` to enforce [D-088](s13-decision-register.md#d-088), and `tests/index_plan_tests.rs` reads `src/temporal/*.rs` back to check the SQL it pins still exists at its source. Those resolve only because the package root *is* the repo root, which is what puts `docs/` inside the `.crate` tarball. One level down, `docs/` falls outside the package and both tests stop compiling for anyone who gets the crate from crates.io.

So the root stays a package *and* becomes a workspace root, with `bindings/python` as its only member. When a workspace root is itself a package, Cargo's `default-members` defaults to that package alone — verified with `cargo metadata`, not assumed — so every bare cargo command means what it meant before, and pyo3 is compiled only when something names it. No CI command uses `--workspace`, so the Rust workflow needed no edit.

**The cost is an `exclude` list, and it was missed on the first pass.** Everything at the repo root ships to crates.io unless named, so `pyproject.toml`, `python/` and `tests_py/` have to be excluded — and shipping them is not an error and raises no warning, so it would have gone unnoticed. `bindings/` needs no entry, because Cargo skips a subdirectory carrying its own `Cargo.toml`. `tests/packaging_tests.rs` pins the list, the absence of `default-members`, and that the root is still a package, since each is one line from being lost with no symptom but a slower build.

A trap worth recording: **`cargo publish` would not catch a bad `include_str!` in `tests/`.** The verify step builds the unpacked tarball; it does not test it. A test reading a file the tarball omits passes `--dry-run` and breaks in a consumer's `cargo test`.

<a id="d-099"></a>D-099 — Every `DbError` variant maps to its own Python exception class with its fields as attributes, and completeness is enforced by an exhaustive `match` rather than by a test (0.7.0). [D-069](s13-decision-register.md#d-069), [D-075](s13-decision-register.md#d-075), [§7](s6-s10-flows-to-dependencies.md#7-errors), [§14.3](s14-python-bindings.md#143-errors).

`DbError` has **27 variants** — the binding plan said 24, and undercounting it is part of why this was worth doing carefully. Several releases were spent making these errors name the right subject: `DiagnosticConn` rather than `NotFound` because a file is not a node; `InvalidId` rather than `NotFound` because refused is not missing; `InvalidTimestamp` rather than `ReplayCorrupt` because bad caller input is not a damaged ledger; `RebuildInterrupted` rather than `RebuildFailed` because *did not run* is not *did not repair*. Rendering all of it onto one class carrying a string would discard that at the last possible moment, in the layer furthest from the reasoning.

So: one class per variant, grouped under `IntegrityError` / `ValidationError` / `VectorError` / `TemporalError` / `WriterError` / `BudgetError` so a caller can catch a family without naming its members, with every field set as an attribute and `str(e)` still the `#[error]` rendering verbatim. Six deliberately separated pairs are pinned as non-inheriting classes, because collapsing one is a regression no functional test would notice — both sides still raise.

**Completeness is the compiler's job, not a test's.** The mapping is a `match` with no wildcard arm, so a variant added upstream fails to build the binding at the line that needs a decision, before any wheel exists. A rule-enforcement test parsing `src/error.rs` — this project's usual instrument, and what the plan specified — can only run after the thing exists, and the failure being guarded against is a new variant falling silently through to the base class, which a wildcard arm would hide. **Verified by injection rather than asserted**: a probe variant added to `DbError` produced `error[E0004]: non-exhaustive patterns … not covered`, pointing at the mapping.

The parsing test is kept for the half a compiler cannot check — that each `setattr` used the right name, that classes sit under the right bases, that they are reachable from the public module — and it closes the seam between the two mechanisms by comparing `src/error.rs` against both the Rust sample table and its own expectation table.

`Overlap` is flattened onto the exception rather than nested behind an `.overlap` object: the seven field names already carry the distinction the type exists to preserve — `valid_*` is what the caller asserted, `existing_*` is what it collided with — so a wrapper adds a hop without adding information.

<a id="d-100"></a>D-100 — Python value types validate in their **constructor**, not at the point of use (0.7.0). [D-029](s13-decision-register.md#d-029), [D-061](s13-decision-register.md#d-061), [§14.4](s14-python-bindings.md#144-timestamps-and-value-types).

`EdgeAssertion::normalized` and `ConceptUpsert::normalized` run on the Rust write path, so a malformed edge type or a second-precision timestamp is a typed error at the call site rather than an engine `CHECK` failure from the far side of the actor. The binding plan carried that arrangement across unchanged, and it is wrong for Python for a reason specific to how these values are used there.

A Python caller builds a **list** and hands it to `write_bulk_atomic` or `write_concepts`. Validating at the write reports *"invalid edge type"* for one of ten thousand edges, with no index and a traceback pointing at the write — where nothing can be fixed. Validating in the constructor puts the failure on the line that built the bad value, which is the line that has to change.

It also makes the types mean something: an `EdgeAssertion` that exists is one the ledger will accept, so the getters can return canonical values rather than whatever was passed, and the write methods cannot fail on shape.

What is **not** tightened: `normalized()` checks timestamp *shape*, not that a date is a real calendar date — `timestamp::parse` does that, later on the write path. So `2026-02-30T…` still constructs. A binding that refused more than the library it binds would be its own kind of surprise, and the boundary is left where the crate put it.

<a id="d-101"></a>D-101 — `Subgraph` crosses as an **opaque handle**, and `to_dict()` is the caller's explicit purchase of a copy (0.7.0). [D-047](s13-decision-register.md#d-047), [D-087](s13-decision-register.md#d-087), [D-097](s13-decision-register.md#d-097), [§14.10](s14-python-bindings.md#1410-the-read-path-and-the-subgraph-handle).

D-097 proposed this; P4.2 delivered it, and the argument survived contact. A `Subgraph` is three `BTreeMap`s and it is the one thing the crate materialises under an explicit `byte_budget`, precisely because it is the largest. Rendering it into Python dicts on return **doubles the peak memory of the single operation that already has a budget** — and does it eagerly, whether or not the caller reads more than `degree()`.

So the accessors are forwarded (`out_edges`, `in_edges`, `degree`, `weighted_degree`, `total_weight`, `edge_count`, `estimated_bytes`, `is_closed`, `node`), plus `__len__`, `__contains__` and iteration over node ids. `to_dict()` exists and is spelled out at the call site.

Two things this settled that D-097 did not anticipate. `is_closed` collides by name with `Database.is_closed` and means something unrelated — the closure invariant, not a shut handle — and the crate's name was kept rather than renamed, because a binding that renames the thing being bound costs every reader who moves between the two APIs. And `__iter__` materialises the id list rather than borrowing the map, because a lazy iterator would hold a borrow across arbitrary Python code; keys are a small fraction of the payload the budget bounded.

<a id="d-102"></a>D-102 — `traverse(attribute_mode=OMIT)` is **refused**, and it is the one place the binding narrows what the library accepts (0.7.0). [D-085](s13-decision-register.md#d-085), [D-100](s13-decision-register.md#d-100), [§14.10](s14-python-bindings.md#1410-the-read-path-and-the-subgraph-handle).

[D-100](s13-decision-register.md#d-100) closes with the rule that a binding refusing more than the library it binds is its own kind of surprise. This is the exception, and the distinction is what the library does with the input.

`normalized()` not checking the calendar is a boundary the crate **chose**; tightening it would surprise someone who read the crate's docs and got a stricter answer. `AttributeMode::Omit` reaching `TraversalBuilder::execute` is a **return type that cannot express the answer** — the crate's own rustdoc says so, and says use `execute_ids` — so it answers `Ok(vec![])`, indistinguishable from a traversal that reached nothing. Forwarding it would re-export a known-ambiguous empty list to callers with strictly less context than a Rust caller has. `traverse_ids()` exists and returns exactly what the mode is for, so the refusal names it.

The related pairing is *not* narrowed: `as_of` without a stated mode raises `AttributeModeUnstatedError` because the ledger raises it, and a live traversal with no `as_of` still defaults, because D-085 made the requirement about the pairing rather than about the mode.

<a id="d-103"></a>D-103 — an unstated `min_weight` on `load_subgraph` is `-inf`, not `0.0` (0.7.0). [D-039](s13-decision-register.md#d-039), [D-073](s13-decision-register.md#d-073), [§14.10](s14-python-bindings.md#1410-the-read-path-and-the-subgraph-handle).

Python has one `load_subgraph`, where Rust has two entry points whose defaults differ — and the difference is load-bearing rather than incidental. `Database::load_subgraph` passes `NEG_INFINITY`; `load_subgraph_with` given a default `TraversalBuilder` passes `0.0`, which **silently drops negative-weight edges**, exactly the input `NegativeEdgeWeight` exists to report given that Dijkstra and A\* are unsound over them.

So the keyword is `min_weight=None` by default and means *unstated*: the floor is `-inf`, and a negative weight reaches the guard. A stated floor **filters**, because a caller who names one has asked to exclude what falls below it, and excluding it is not an error. `traverse` keeps the builder's `0.0`, because there a weight filter is only a filter.

**A measured consequence worth recording**: as of 0.6.0 that guard is unreachable from a ledger this binding wrote. [D-083](s13-decision-register.md#d-083) put `CHECK (weight >= 0.0 …)` on `links`, so the row is refused at the write. It stays reachable for a file migrated from before v7 — `links_current` carries no weight check of its own — which is why the mapping was kept rather than simplified on the strength of a constraint that only holds for new writes.

<a id="d-104"></a>D-104 — `astar` holds the GIL for its whole run, and a misbehaving heuristic is captured and re-raised (0.7.0). [D-095](s13-decision-register.md#d-095), [§14.12](s14-python-bindings.md#1412-the-algorithms-and-astars-inversion).

The bindings plan flagged this as the one method needing its own design pass, because an arbitrary Python callable means calling **into** Python from Rust while [§14.2](s14-python-bindings.md#142-the-asyncsync-boundary)'s arrangement has the GIL released. The fallback it proposed — ship `astar` with a fixed heuristic set instead of a callable — was not needed.

**It does not release the GIL, and that is the resolution rather than a compromise.** Releasing it around the search and re-attaching per expansion pays two GIL transitions per node to hold it for the arithmetic in between: strictly worse than never releasing it. Every other algorithm *does* detach — they are pure CPU over Rust-owned data with no Python object in reach — so the cost is isolated to the one method that earns it, and it is documented at the method: `heuristic=None` and `dijkstra` both release.

Two hazards the callback signature creates, because `Fn(&str, &str) -> f64` cannot report failure:

- **A raising heuristic** would otherwise be swallowed. The error is captured in a `RefCell` and re-raised after the search returns; `0.0` is fed to the search meanwhile, which is admissible on every graph, so the search stays well-defined and terminates rather than running on a poisoned ordering.
- **A `NaN`** makes the priority queue's comparison incoherent and panics — *inside a callback, across an FFI boundary*, which is a far worse outcome than an exception. Non-finite returns are refused by name, and the message says which node.

`heuristic=None` is the default and means a zero heuristic: the one heuristic admissible on every graph, which makes the default A\* exactly Dijkstra-with-a-path rather than a guess about the caller's metric space.

<a id="d-105"></a>D-105 — search results are a `VectorHit` class, while edges stay tuples (0.7.0). [D-007](s13-decision-register.md#d-007), [§14.11](s14-python-bindings.md#1411-temporal-vector-and-the-counters).

Not a style preference, and the two choices are consistent under one rule: a tuple is fine when every slot means the same kind of thing across every producer.

An edge tuple's five slots are identifiers and instants in every context, so `e[3]` is a `valid_from` wherever it came from. A search result's `score` is a **cosine distance** from `search_vector` (smaller is closer, ascending), a **fused RRF score** from `hybrid_search` (larger is better, descending), and *bm25* from `keyword_search` (negative, ascending best-first). Three incompatible orderings behind one position, and `hit[1]` invites sorting the wrong way. A named field with a docstring does not prevent that; it gives the caller somewhere to look when the top result is the worst match.

`keyword_search` keeps `(id, rank)` tuples, because it is the single arm whose ordering is fixed and stated at its own call.

`CandidateCount` is **not** exposed as a type. Its whole content is a number and whether that number is exact, which is two attributes on `CostEstimate` — `candidates` and `candidates_capped`. A class would make a caller unwrap it to reach the number they wanted, which is how the cap ends up ignored, and the cap is the part that matters: SQLite has no histograms, so the count is measured by running the traversal under a probe, and a probe that hit its cap measured only that the set is bigger than the cap.

<a id="d-106"></a>D-106 — one abi3 wheel per platform, an sdist as the universal fallback, and PyPI uploads by Trusted Publishing rather than a token (0.7.0). [D-094](s13-decision-register.md#d-094), [D-098](s13-decision-register.md#d-098), [§14.13](s14-python-bindings.md#1413-packaging-and-distribution).

**The matrix is small because [D-094](s13-decision-register.md#d-094) made it small.** `pyo3` is built `abi3-py310`, so one wheel per platform serves CPython 3.10 and everything after it. Without abi3 this is four platforms times five interpreter versions — twenty builds of a crate that compiles the SQLite amalgamation each time. That is the other half of a trade whose visible cost was ~35 µs per 768-dim embedding, and it is the half that pays for it.

**Probe P5-a is answered for one target and open for the rest.** Cold, `cargo clean` first, on Windows x86_64: **54–62 s, 197 crates compiled including `libsql-ffi`**; the wheel is **4.3 MiB compressed, 11.0 MiB unpacked**, of which the extension is 10.7 MiB. The sdist is 748 KiB, and `pip install --no-binary :all:` on it takes **183 s** in a fresh virtualenv. So the build is not expensive and the plan's instruction not to assume the matrix is cheap is discharged — *for the native case*. The stated risk was aarch64 under emulation, typically 5–15× native, which is affordable but unmeasured; the first run of `wheels.yml` is what measures it, and the fallback if it does not fit is a native arm64 runner rather than dropping the target.

**The sdist is not a formality.** It is the install path for every platform the matrix does not cover, so the CI job proves it *builds from source with no wheel in reach* (`--no-binary :all:`) rather than proving a tarball was produced. This works because `libsql-ffi` compiles the amalgamation anyway and needs only a C compiler — verified locally end to end, including that the `metrics` feature survives the source build, which is what makes `Database.metrics()` honest in a wheel-less install too.

**`musllinux` stays out of scope** until someone asks: it doubles the Linux matrix for a `libsql-ffi` build nobody here has checked against musl, and an untested wheel is worse than an sdist that works.

**No API token exists for this.** PyPI uploads use Trusted Publishing — OIDC, a short-lived credential minted for one repository and one workflow — so there is no long-lived secret to leak, rotate, or hand to anyone, and the project owner configures it once on PyPI itself. `tests_py/test_packaging.py` asserts the workflow references no secret, because the easy fix for a failed upload is to paste a token in and that change looks small in review.

**The naming risk is recorded rather than designed around.** Distribution `macrame-db`, import `macrame`, mirroring the crate — and unlike Rust's per-build-graph `[lib] name`, `site-packages` is flat, so a second distribution installing a top-level `macrame/` would contend for the directory. The incumbent is a dead 2021 build tool and `pip` warns on file conflicts, so the risk is known and non-silent. `macrame_db` is the fallback if it ever materialises, and the README now says all of this next to the crate-name note.

<a id="d-107"></a>D-107 — the Python suite is gated by `run_suite.py`, not by pytest's exit code, and the R15 reporting hazard is **not** the same on both sides (0.7.0). [R15](s11-s12-milestones-and-risks.md#12-risks), [D-092](s13-decision-register.md#d-092), [§14.7](s14-python-bindings.md#147-r15-reaches-through-the-binding).

[§14.7](s14-python-bindings.md#147-r15-reaches-through-the-binding), `pyproject.toml` and `tests_py/conftest.py` all stated that the Rust reporting hazard "carries across unchanged". **P6 measured it and it does not.** The fault is the same; the reporting is not, because the two suites have different process topologies, and the guidance that followed from the assumption was wrong in both directions.

`cargo test` runs **one process per test binary**. A crash removes that binary's tests from the aggregate while every other binary still prints its summary, so the run reads as a *smaller pass count with no failures* — green, and wrong. That is the hazard as originally written and it is correct for Rust.

pytest runs **one process**. Measured with a deliberate `faulthandler._sigsegv()` mid-suite: **exit code 3, and no summary line at all**. So the original advice — "key on the summary line being present, not on the exit code alone" — describes a check that would work, for a reason that does not apply: on this side the exit code alone *would* have caught it.

What the exit code would not catch is the inverse, and that one is specific to this extension. `Drop for PyDatabase` enters the tokio runtime ([§14.2](s14-python-bindings.md#142-the-asyncsync-boundary)), so a fault during interpreter teardown lands **after** pytest prints its green summary: a passing suite with a non-zero exit. There, reading only the summary is wrong and reading only the exit code is right by accident.

`tests_py/run_suite.py` therefore checks the summary, the failure count, the collected count and the exit code **against each other**, and names four distinguishable outcomes — `CRASH`, `FAILED`, `INCOMPLETE`, `TEARDOWN`. Only `CRASH` is retried, three times, matching `ci.yml`'s Rust retry and for the same stated reason: R15 has always passed on re-run, so a genuine failure still goes red, and re-running anything else until it is green is how a flaky assertion becomes permanent. A `TEARDOWN` result is deliberately red rather than retried — the tests passed and the process still died, which is a defect in the shutdown path rather than in an assertion.

**Verified by injection in both directions**, which is the only way a gate is worth anything: an injected segfault retries three times and reports `CRASH`; an injected failing assertion reports `FAILED` on the first attempt and does not retry.

Probe P6-a is closed by P1's measurement rather than by new work: `tests_py/probes/r15_concurrent_open.py` faulted **2 in 12 runs** at 48 concurrent opens, the same rate as the Rust control arm, so the boundary is transparent to R15 and the single-process constraint is measured rather than inherited.

<a id="d-108"></a>D-108 — the Python workflow does **not** gate on `ci.yml`, and it is the first thing in this repository that compiles the binding crate (0.7.0). [D-098](s13-decision-register.md#d-098), [D-106](s13-decision-register.md#d-106), [D-107](s13-decision-register.md#d-107), [§14.14](s14-python-bindings.md#1414-ci).

The binding plan sketched `python.yml` as `rust: uses ./.github/workflows/ci.yml` with everything else behind `needs: rust` — the shape `release.yml` uses. That shape belonged to a file that *also built the wheels*, and the rationale was "the crate must be green before the wheel is built". P5 moved the wheel matrix into its own tag-triggered `wheels.yml`, and what remained here is a test job whose failure mode is independent of the Rust suite's.

`workflow_call` does not deduplicate. Gating would re-run clippy, MSRV, the two-OS Rust matrix — each arm with its own three R15 retries — and the publish dry-run on every pull request that already ran all of them, and would hold the Python answer behind work it does not depend on. Two independent signals reported separately is strictly more information than one signal reported late. Where the two **must** be green together is before an upload, and that is where the gate went: `wheels.yml`'s publish job calls this workflow, exactly as `release.yml` calls `ci.yml`. Before P7 a tag could build four wheels, pass a six-line smoke test and upload to PyPI with the suite never having run, on a version number that cannot be spent twice.

**The gap this closed was not in the plan at all.** `bindings/python` is a workspace *member* but never a *default* member — the root package is itself a member, so Cargo scopes bare commands to it alone. That is deliberate, it is what keeps `cargo publish` a one-package operation ([D-098](s13-decision-register.md#d-098)), and `tests/packaging_tests.rs` pins it.
